ParlaMint-ES_2015-10-01-CD151001.u97	Thank you, Mr. President. Ladies and gentlemen, Minister, first of all I would like to welcome the deans of prosecutors who are following this debate from the rostrum. No effort is left when it comes to Spain and the Spanish. Since the beginning of this legislature, the Government has worked hard and hard to improve the agility, quality, efficiency of justice and its vocation to serve society, and the People's Parliamentary Group, which supports this Government, has been actively involved in this ambitious plan of legislative reforms; only in the last eleven months have 14 bills been processed, and in these sessions we have already adopted the last ones. This is the only way to strengthen the rule of law, increase legal certainty and strengthen our competitiveness as a country. Do you know, ladies and gentlemen, that for two years we have been increasing the budget and placing justice as one of the main priorities? That's right. The budget of the Ministry of Justice increased in 2015, and will increase again by 7.2% in 2016. In addition, investments will increase in the coming year, no less than 26% more than for this course, especially for the technological modernisation of the justice system. With more and better laws, more means, and more technological endowments, we will make it possible for all of us to have justice more agile and faster, because, as Seneca said, nothing resembles injustice with late justice. And precisely the reform of the Civil Prosecution Act 1/2000 of 7 January, which we are debating today, and which is still in time to support all groups, will contribute to the modernization of justice, which is not merely a declaration of intent, because there are very real objectives and detailed planning. I turn to the specific aspects of the reform. The first and most important is precisely the promotion of new technologies. How do we get a more agile and modern justice? Accelerating, among other things, the use of new technologies and telematics. We must adapt to the new demands. Modernisation is a process and it has no backsliding. At first there are resistances and difficulties, because it is a real revolution. For example, after the dye and the pen came a huge change, at the beginning of the 20th century, with the typewriter, and there have been mechanical, electrical and electronic ones - there are still some in the courts -. Now it's about making the paper disappear and moving on to the electronic file, another great revolution. If that typewriter contributed to improving and modernizing us, the current technological leap is infinitely greater, and consequently the benefits as well. We can't stay behind. We are in the twenty-first century and we need justice from the twenty-first century. January 1, 2016 will be a reality: the courts will work exclusively through electronic means. The paperless justice is one of the objectives, and this is possible, and it is especially up to the prosecutors of the courts, a figure perhaps unknown, but indispensable. They are the ones who really know the process and assume its management. In this section I would also like to mention the use of that secure exchange platform, which is LexNET. In the face of skeptics, the General Council of Prosecutors of Spain offers us the following data. If in 2004 they processed 35,664 notifications with LexNET, in 2010 there were already 11,300,000 and in 2014 - last updated data - four times more, in particular 46,293,000. And it is that prosecutors are the best allies for the modernization of justice. These data show that the Administration of Justice is sufficiently qualified to comply with this obligation to use telematics systems since 1 January 2016. A second aspect of the reform: greater involvement of prosecutors within the administration of justice. It is desirable that the prosecutor may also take on other tasks of cooperation with the courts and lawyers, in particular in the context of the acts of communication, in the procedural stages of proof and execution, and in the systems of forcible sale of goods under the terms and with the limitations laid down. That is not what we say, this is an orientation of the White Paper on Justice, drawn up by the General Council of the Judiciary, which highlighted it. Revolution therefore in the judicial system, continued with the change undertaken by the Government since 2011, and with that consideration that the council gave us in 1998. Be bothered to read and understand that book, and if you have read it again with the intention of discovering what is stated there. This is not about privatizing justice. It's not. What do we achieve by assigning new functions to prosecutors? That auxiliaries or secretaries can devote more time to other tasks, and therefore it can be said that resources are increased in the field of justice, the options of the justiciable are improved, and in their area of will there remains the decision whether to take advantage of the system designed, without, ladies and gentlemen, implying a two-speed justice or privatization by the Administration of Justice with the acts of communication. The other three pillars of the reform of the Civil Prosecution Act are the improvement of the verbal judgment, a greater defence of consumers against unfair clauses, not to mention changes in free legal aid. The verbal trial reforms. After fifteen years of practical implementation of the Civil Prosecution Act, the different legal operators asked us for a change in the regulation of the verbal trial, in order precisely to strengthen the effective judicial guardianship. What is the purpose? The aim is nothing other than to avoid the defencelessness of the parties, allowing them to act with equal arms and be able to go to trial with the necessary means of proof, without delay in their processing. The written reply to the application is introduced to give greater certainty to the proceedings, and the summons of unnecessary witnesses will also be avoided if the expert judicial evidence is made viable without the need to suspend the hearing, as is currently the case. It is precisely the opposite of what some of the groups have wanted to see; for example, they have not understood that when the parties request that they have not seen and the judge considers it appropriate, rather than harming the citizen, what it does is to offer him more advantages, such as the reduction of the time of judicial response or the saving of the costs of the procedure. The last aspect is the change in the control of unfair clauses in the monitoring procedure. This control is improved by allowing the judge to assess whether there are such clauses, and therefore there is an improvement in the defence of consumers and users. Finally, changes in free legal aid. These are improvements, ladies and gentlemen, which extend the subjective scope of the law, since new subjects can access it, and the objective scope, since new situations are likely to have recognized this right. Among the new subjects who can access free legal aid, I would like to highlight the associations of victims of terrorism and large families, who have that status because of the special circumstances of their members and not because of the number of them. Ladies and gentlemen, the passage of this law through the Senate has greatly improved its content. In particular, twenty-three articles, one additional provision, two transitional provisions and three final articles have been amended in the Upper House. By highlighting one of the amendments included in the draft, I will mention the one made to article 14, paragraph 2, by which the time allowed to the defendant to respond to the claim is not suspended by the decision of the court secretary, but by the submission of the application, and the other is the possibility that the descendants of lawyers may claim the fees that would not have been met. During the parliamentary process of this law we have had to listen unfortunately to many barbarities such as, for example, that prosecutors are enemies of the Administration of Justice or that justice will be privatized with this reform, or even here two bills have been mixed, the Criminal Prosecution Act and the Civil Prosecution Act. However, this is a law, Mrs Ramon - and I come back to the beginning - which is essential to give greater agility and quality to our justice; a law that is the fruit of the work and effort of many people, of encounters with different collectives, with all legal operators. We have never wanted this law to be the law of the People's Party, but the law of all, because the prevailing spirit has been that of negotiation. We are therefore grateful for all the contributions of all the speakers from the various parliamentary groups. And I do not want to finish my speech without showing my thanks to my two fellow rapporteurs, Mr Molinero Hoyos and Mr Martín-Toledano, for the magnificent work done, for their professionalism and good doing; to the lawyers of the House, for their technical remarks, and to the minister here today and to his team, whom I also see in the rostrum. He sees justice as a matter of state, and that has resulted in legislative and structural reforms undertaken based on the adaptation of new technologies, process improvements and the increase of human and material resources. This will help the Justice Administration to stop being perceived by the citizen as that slow machine that Hamlet was already talking about in his monologue. Today we have taken one more step among all of us. Thank you very much, Mr President. Thank you, ladies and gentlemen, for your attention. [[One Member: Very well!-Applause]]

As you can imagine, I am not going to talk about the aspects that we consider to be positive, but it is time to highlight those aspects that should have been improved throughout the parliamentary process. The Government has amended the text of the bill to ensure that the cassation effectively fulfils its unifying function of criminal doctrine. In this section we consider that, in addition to a centralizing manoeuvre by the Government, which delegates provincial hearings to establish their doctrinal criteria, this reform misses the opportunity to adjust the regulation of autonomous statutes. In particular, greater powers should be conferred on the relevant higher courts of justice, in accordance with the provisions of the statutes of autonomy. This is the case with the Statute of Autonomy of Catalonia. Another aspect of concern to us is that the legal aid of the accused is not adequately guaranteed in all proceedings and that the right of defence of the accused is not protected. Despite the fact that both draft laws state in their preamble that such reforms are planned in order to strengthen the procedural rights of defendants and detainees, the legislative development of these fundamental rights is not at all guaranteed and is imprecise and lacking in content in terms of practical foresight during criminal proceedings. We have moved this in the form of amendments which have not been incorporated into the text. For example, aspects such as the requirement for legal aid during all proceedings are essential. Counselling should take place not only during the judicial disposition or appearance before the judge, but should be extended. The detainee must be able to be assisted from the moment of arrest in police proceedings, as well as throughout the judicial process. Another important aspect is to ensure the defence of the detainee, and the lawyer is able to examine all the proceedings. It is essential for the lawyer to have access to all information in police and judicial proceedings in order to be able to provide adequate assistance to the detainee and to guarantee the right to effective judicial protection. With regard to the intervention of communications in the event of an emergency, we do not agree with the adoption, without due judicial authorization, of measures which may restrict fundamental rights, as it is contrary to the current legal order and may lead to excessive or fraudulent practices. We believe that it is necessary to abolish the power of the police to place a technical monitoring and tracing device or means without judicial authorization in an emergency. Finally, another important aspect is the provision of material and personal resources to enable the Administration of Justice to proceed with the new measures provided for by the reform of the Criminal Prosecution Act. This - we believe - is a fundamental aspect: to provide adequate means for the implementation of the measures contained precisely in these projects. Thank you very much.

That is why we reform a law of the nineteenth century, when what we need is a procedural law of the twenty-first century, a law that finally attributes the investigation to the prosecutor's office and places the judge in his role as a guarantor judge, as a guarantee judge, according to the constitutional model. It is therefore a very partial reform, a reform to a large extent improvised and full of contradictions. On the one hand, the model is maintained - of course, the model is not changed - that the investigating judge is the one who drives, the one who directs, the one who controls the investigation - the model of the investigating judge is maintained and the investigation and the investigation is not given to the prosecutor's office - and, on the other hand, in some of the key reforms that you, Minister, have brought to this House, functions of impulse and direction of the proceedings contradictory with which they are now taken away from the investigating judge are attributed to the prosecutor's office. They are two different models, two contradictory models that mix inadequately and perhaps recklessly in the results of this reform and which will soon have to be overcome. ParlaMint-ES_2015-10-01-CD151001.u127	Thank you very much, sincerely, Mr. De la Rocha. For the People's Group, Mr Ordóñez has the floor.
ParlaMint-ES_2015-10-01-CD151001.u128	Madam President, ladies and gentlemen, at the beginning of my speech, I would like to thank the other two rapporteurs of my group, Mr José Antonio Bermúdez and Mr Agustín Conde, for the enormous work they have done with these two bills, and the Minister of Justice, here today, for the impetus given to a reform that contributes so much to the improvement of the criminal process and to the defence of citizens' rights. I would also like to thank the other groups for their remarkable contribution. Ladies and gentlemen, we are celebrating this last parliamentary debate on justice and we are doing so with the deep conviction that we have succeeded in ensuring that around thirty initiatives will enable all citizens to exercise their rights better. The adaptation of a rule such as the Criminal Prosecution Act of 1882 to the reality of the year 2015 is a necessary consequence of the passage of time, but it is also the duty of the State, of which this House is the fundamental pillar, to establish agile, modern justice that strengthens the procedural guarantees of democratic regeneration and the fight against corruption. This reform is articulated in two draft laws: one, of a regular nature, with a new and updated regulation of the connection, the attestations without known author, the time limits of investigation, the appeal, the process by acceptance of decree, confiscation or the remedy of revision; and another, of an organic nature, necessary for affecting fundamental rights, which regulates such important matters as the status of the investigated person, the undercover agent, technological investigation measures or replacement of accused person by the investigated or prosecuted person. Two are therefore the clear objectives of this reform: the speeding up of criminal justice and the eradication of the endemic evil of delays in processing. For this purpose, the instructional times are changed. First, the ineffective one-month period is replaced by the six-month period for the instruction of simple cases and eighteen months for complex cases. This will allow the injured person to obtain without further delay the just reparation, but also to prevent a delay from entailing a penalty added to the guilty party or an undeserved stigmatization of the innocent. Some point out that the setting of time limits causes defencelessness in the field of evidence. We strongly say that no, since the investigation proceedings agreed on in time will be valid without prejudice to their subsequent incorporation into the proceedings. Nor impunity, because, in fact, if the deadline is over, the cause is over, but if there are, even if the deadline is over, it will continue until the oral trial. This is expressly provided for in article 324.8. The rules of connectivity are also modified. As your honors know, the instructor was obliged to investigate in a single process all the various crimes against the same person if it involved some kind of similarity or analogy, origin and reason of the endless causes that we all know. This reform is based on the principle of 'a crime, a cause'. In future, the accumulation by connection will take place only in cases expressly assessed. The procedure for accepting a decree, the so-called criminal monitoring, is also introduced, which will allow a prompt response to those offences punishable by fine, works for the benefit of the community or imprisonment not exceeding one year, replaced by a fine with or without deprivation of driving licence; a procedure already consolidated in comparative law and which finds its reason in the principle of procedural economy, both for the State and for the defendant himself, who is also allowed to reduce by a third his sentence with the procedural guarantee that the appearance must be registered in full, if possible, by audiovisual means, and if not, must be documented with the subjection to the general rules. The obligation to refer the attestations without known author to the court is abolished, and we have to say that 40% of the attestations do not go beyond the criminal relevance. Therefore, a procedure is abolished, which only makes it unnecessary to accumulate judicial proceedings; that is, it is safeguarded and requires referral in those crimes against life, physical integrity, sexual freedom and compensation or those crimes related to corruption. In addition, the protection of the injured person is maintained in full, since at the time of the preparation of the attestation he is warned that if in seventy-two hours the attested author is not known, he will be archived. But it is also possible, both in the Prosecution Act and in the victim's statute, to have access at any time to a judicial presence or to the Ministry of Public Prosecutions. It must also be said that, of course, the certificates will not be destroyed and will always be available to the prosecutor's office and the judicial authority. The second aspect of this reform is to strengthen procedural guarantees and promote legal certainty. First, the word imputed is replaced by the word investigated or, at a later stage, prosecuted. Ladies and gentlemen, we are all aware that the word imputed had achieved a sort of social meaning as almost responsible: the person to whom an act is attributed even before finding out whether that act was possible. While investigating is the possibility of knowing, imputing is already ascribe a certain behavior to a person. Ladies and gentlemen, we cannot say that we safeguard the right of presumption of innocence if society stigmatises a citizen even before it warns of anything against him. This reform also establishes a new statute for the investigator, with the right to a reserved interview with his/her lawyer even before he/she is received a statement before the judicial police, prosecutor's office or judicial authority. Confidentiality of communications with counsel, third-party information of detention or detention abroad or remoteness, which is also regulated in the protection of the person concerned. Protection of the most vulnerable detainees, minors or persons with judicially modified capacities. The regulation of DNA sampling. The incommunicado period of detention is limited and restricted to the strictly necessary time, with a limit of five days, which can be extended to ten days, and also with a decision motivated by the judicial authority. The physical integrity and indemnity of the person are fully preserved by requiring that the person be recognized as a medical practitioner, at least twice every 24 hours, according to the discretion of the medical practitioner. The general reform also affects the establishment of the second instance and there is also a new regulation on the appeal procedure. We welcome the intervention of Mr Emilio Olabría, as we welcome the intervention by action or omission of the other speakers, who in no way criticize the content of the reform, although they put some obstacles - something obvious, because we are talking about the opposition groups - in terms of its processing or incorporation. The truth is that in substance I have not heard, except for some error that I might have, any reproach as to the content and introduction of the cassation remedy in this new regulation. Similarly, the review appeal is established to enforce the judgements of the European Court of Human Rights, and, very important, to deal with democratic regeneration and the fight against corruption, a process of autonomous confiscation is regulated that will allow the State to appropriate property derived from the crime, even if the perpetrator cannot be tried. On 29 July, Fr. Pedro Sánchez called a press conference to announce that if he ever came to the government, he would reform the Criminal Code to act against the assets of the corrupt, even if they had it on behalf of third parties. How sorry we were that he did not know that that was precisely what he had voted against on 26 March; he and the whole Socialist Group, like the Minister of Justice, not without sadness, needed to remember him. Ladies and gentlemen, you may still be assimilating the ever-illustrated verb of Don Emilio Olabría on the Marquis of Beccaria and the ontological foundation of the penalties, or perhaps embroiled in how to incorporate into the debate his recurring mantras of austerity, cuts, the right, privatizations or the suffering of the humble. There you go. I would ask you now, knowing what you are going to vote on, to supplement the reform of the Criminal Code on 26 March, to instruct Don Pedro to support a reform that protects and enables society to be compensated for the effects of crime, but which also represents a major step forward in the fundamental rights of the detainee. This is something to which we all have to feel committed, as were the opposition groups with their contributions in the ordinary law by regulating the attestations without known author, the setting of deadlines, the monitoring, the confiscation or the remedy of review; or in the organic law, with the prior interview, the undercover agent, the protection of the right to honour, privacy or image, or in the regulation of the right to information on the occasion of an arrest. We are, ladies and gentlemen, facing a reform that responds to the cry and the right of all...

ParlaMint-ES_2015-10-01-CD151001.u134	We move on to the next item on the agenda. Amendments adopted by the Senate to the draft Organic Law of the Military Penal Code. Mrs Lozano has the floor for the Parliamentary Group on Progressive Union and Democracy to set positions.
ParlaMint-ES_2015-10-01-CD151001.u135	Thank you very much, President. Since the beginning of this bill, my group explained that it seemed a mistake to us, because the truly relevant debate that needs to be addressed about military jurisdiction is the relevance of its continuing existence. We have already explained on numerous occasions, during this procedure, that the point of view of our parliamentary group is that military justice should be abolished. Not only was it pointless to stir up this debate in this legislature, but it had to be shut down through a reform of the Military Penal Code, which was an involution. We have explained why military jurisdiction makes the Armed Forces a territory of emergency, opacity, a territory in which corporatism prevails over justice in the end and where it is often much easier to find cases of impunity, lack of guarantees and even deprivation of the rights of the accused than cases of true justice. Military courts operate on numerous occasions as genuine military units because they are not independent courts. That is why my group has insisted on the need, like so many other countries - Germany, Norway - to dispense with that military jurisdiction. From that point of view, all this processing, which has been a mistake, concludes today, but does not conclude the error, and its consequences will continue to be suffered for years by many men and women who provide a huge service to Spain and who do not deserve criminal treatment such as that provided by this Penal Code. The second option, since the Government was unwilling to abolish that military jurisdiction, was to reduce the list of crimes, because it did not see the relationship between theft and the Army, for example, or between sexual harassment and the Army. Common crimes must be tried within the ordinary jurisdiction and, in any case, be restricted to the minimum of what remains within the scope of military jurisdiction. But far from addressing this second approach, what the Government is doing with this reform is expanding that catalogue of crimes. That is why we have explained - the case of sexual harassment within the army is very revealing - how to define it as a crime does not contribute to its persecution, to its disappearance as a practice, but rather that the necessary thing is prevention and, above all, to guarantee the victims safe channels to report. To include it as a crime simply means to extend that military jurisdiction that perpetuates that territory of exception, which in the legal sphere is the Armed Forces. None of this has changed in the handling of the law in the Senate. There has been no evolution in military justice at all, but, on the contrary, there is an involution. We will therefore once again vote against these amendments. Thank you, President.
ParlaMint-ES_2015-10-01-CD151001.u136	Thank you very much, Mrs. Lozano. For the UI Group, ICV-EUiA, CHA: The Plural Left, Mr Sanz has the floor.
ParlaMint-ES_2015-10-01-CD151001.u137	Thank you, President. Today the proceedings of this debate on the Military Penal Code and for the Group of the Plural Left are completed. There are the same doubts, concerns and considerations and opinions that we have already expressed throughout their proceedings since consideration. What goes wrong ends badly, and this debate about the Military Penal Code began badly, mainly because it was born behind the back of society, without any analysis at all and at any time of the opinion of the military, of the people, of the men and women who make up the Armed Forces, disdaining or, worse still, not even taking into account what they thought and what their professional organizations and associations thought. Once again, we must remember the complaints that have been made to this effect by these military associations and the Civil Guard - and I shall speak of that later - which have repeatedly said that this rule had not been conceived and arose without the necessary debate and careful analysis that it required. Today this House will adopt a new military penal rule that increases, as has already been said, the number of military criminal types and does so perversely because only the number of articles is apparently thinned and condenses that increase into a single article. That's what happens with this new rule, with this new penal code: the articles go down, but the criminal rates go up. They do so, moreover, beyond the military, affecting the crimes that already have their correlative characterization in the field of ordinary justice, in the Common Criminal Code, that is, overcoming and exceeding the constitutional mandate to refer and strictly limit themselves to the castress sphere and again demonstrating, in a clear way, the ideology and the conception that the People's Party and the Government have, in this case, of the rule of law. A separate mention requires the treatment given to the Civil Guard; this strange obsessiveness that the Government has to apply to the Civil Guard the military penal norm, despite the contrary opinion of all groups of the House; despite the contrary opinion of all associations of the Civil Guard professionals. You maintain the spirit of Article 1(5) and consolidate and increase the application of this code to the Civil Guard. We have already warned you that we are not going to vote on any of the amendments that have to do with this matter because they are doing nothing other than consolidating this genuine misconception, ladies and gentlemen of the People's Party. It is a very important legal error; a nonsense, moreover, that will allow a few to apply this penal rule of a military nature to the Civil Guard in its daily activity, an activity that is police, always police, at all times, in all circumstances, in fulfilment of the constitutional mission that have the same, common and identical as that assigned to the National Police by the Constitution. I speak of the daily and normal exercise of the functions that the body performs. Who wins with this mistake, ladies and gentlemen of the People's Party? It is clear that the same ones that justify that a civil authority, such as the Director General of the Civil Guard, is now considered a military authority and has a special criminal protection that he did not previously have and that had not been necessary for decades. Who loses? How does this affect the daily work of the men and women of the Civil Guard? Obviously in a very negative way, because they are going to be charged and charged with crimes, for behaviors in their daily activity than in the best of cases, and if there are reasons for this, they must be assessed first in the disciplinary field. In another matter, we are concerned about the criminalization of the criticism that this wording allows and we again denounce the wording of article 37, as we have already done in the Commission and put it back on the table, because we believe that it is a very dangerous door to punish the representatives and leaders of professional associations when they criticize situations - which there are, on a daily basis - that occur within the Armed Forces and now in the Civil Guard. All this, moreover, without the reform of military jurisdiction, a process that is too late for two years and conditioned, in any case, on partial reforms that are made to give an appearance of modernity, but that deepen in a model of military justice away from ordinary justice. That is the central element of this debate, which we have closed, wrongly, today: the confrontation of two models in the field of military criminal justice. A model that is defended by the Government, the People's Party, the Army's major states and hierarchies, which is the one that inspires and collects the text, which is based on the maintenance of the functioning of military jurisdiction, its organs, its regulation, its powers, its territorial scope, its procedural rules and the nature and origin of its judges. A model which, therefore, involves reproducing the mistakes of the past which, in the absence of impartiality, independence and taxonomy, is absolutely unacceptable in a criminal law. The other model, which is the alternative model that defends The Plural Left, is a model that is based on the overcoming and disappearance of military jurisdiction, circumscribing military criminal laws to certain conjunctures of the State, of the internal life of the State, that is, state of alarm, emergency, site, declared war. Finally, it is a model of the reality of the twenty-first century, of a modern society, of a democratic society that wants to have professional armed forces that carry out their work with full guarantees of respect for their fundamental rights and personal and public freedoms; a model, ladies and gentlemen, which involves among other issues - nor is it addressed in this reform - dealing with the procedural field as, on the other hand, it also determines the Law on Rights and Obligations. You, ladies and gentlemen of the People's Party, have avoided this debate and have rejected each of the proposals that you wanted or intended to deepen on this issue, so our vote is going to be a negative vote on this law, which is going to be passed in addition to a few weeks from the next election date. We hope that after that election, in a few months you will vacate that bench, leave the Government, necessarily be evicted from the Government, in order to reopen this debate in order to ensure, once and for all, that justice and democracy are not left at the door of the barracks. ParlaMint-ES_2015-10-01-CD151001.u141	Thank you very much, Madam President. Ladies and gentlemen, our parliamentary group is going to cast a negative vote on the amendment to article 1.5 of the bill of the Military Penal Code that comes from the Senate. This is the amendment that is being debated this afternoon, which is much more than just an amendment, because it is nothing more and nothing less than to clarify how the Military Penal Code applies to the whole of the Civil Guard, to a security corps as important and valued as this. In the previous socialist legislature there was a consensus on this - I would like to recall it because it is very appropriate in this debate - and a consensus was reached through Article 7a - which is currently in force - of the Military Penal Code, which was voted by the People's Party, on the application of the Military Penal Code to the Civil Guard. I have here the text before this article 7bis, which says that the Military Penal Code will not apply when the Civil Guard carries out acts proper to its mission, to protect the free exercise of rights and freedoms and to guarantee citizen security. However, the Penal Code does apply when the Civil Guard operates in time of war or state of siege, military missions or is integrated into military units; in that case, the Penal Code does apply. That was the criterion of this amendment to the Military Penal Code in the previous legislature, which adopted a number of parliamentary groups, including the People's Group and the Socialist Group. The basis for this change that was made some years ago is the nature of the Civil Guard, a subject to which Mr. Guillaumes referred. The Civil Guard has a nature of police corps, of security corps. The functions of the Civil Guard are 95% police, civilian and security missions. That is their true nature; a nature that is, as it were, the background of the subject. The role of the Civil Guard is at the heart of the issue, that's really important, in what benefits citizens the role they play. What citizens want is to have effective results in the security forces and they do not care so much about the internal and disciplinary regime. They care about the effectiveness in this case of the Civil Guard, that's the bottom. The way in the whole history of the Civil Guard is to have a military disciplinary regime; that's the way. But the bottom influences the shape. We can't separate them at all. That is why the internal operating structure of the Civil Guard is not the same as that of the Army, it is not part of the Army and it does not have the same structure. He said earlier, I think with wise words, Mr. Guillaumes that the way a police officer operates is not the same as that of a military officer. He's explained it very well. That is why there are changes in the organizational structure of the Civil Guard that are not the same as what happens in the Armed Forces. I will cite two examples from the previous legislature. One, the suppression of the arrest warrants. The Civil Guard has no sanction of arrest, under its disciplinary regime the sanctions of arrest have disappeared, the deprivation of liberty has disappeared as an internal sanction and this has not produced any less effectiveness of the Civil Guard. I imagine that you will not agree to say that the Civil Guard is less effective, that it has lowered its effectiveness. I do not think that that opinion could be shared, even though arrests as an internal sanction system have disappeared from the Civil Guard, even for serious offences. However, there is an arrest penalty in the Armed Forces, even for minor offences. I repeat, in the Civil Guard, not even for serious faults; in the Army, even for minor faults. The other element that gives it a different form of functioning was precisely this article 7bis to which I referred earlier, and that is that in the previous legislature it was concluded that the Military Criminal Code, as a general rule, should not apply to the Civil Guard, which does not mean that there would be impunity because it would be governed by the ordinary Criminal Code and its hypothetical criminal actions would be judged by civilian judges, not military judges. This conclusion was reached that the Military Criminal Code was not applied to the Civil Guard as a general rule. What does the Government Bill do, the one that the Government sent last year? What article 1.5 does is to change and reverse that rule, and to say: as a general rule, the Military Penal Code will apply, with certain exceptions. It reverses the rule, the general rule goes from the non-applies to the self-applies, which is a very important change in philosophy. In our view it is an involution and that is why we, before the summer, when there was a debate on it, voted against Article 1.5. I then asked Mr Ferrer - he is still in time to answer me - why the Government sent us this bill with Article 1.5 breaking the consensus of Article 7bis still in force in the previous legislature. Why? What is the reason for moving from consensus to dissent when there was a very important agreement in this area between the large parliamentary groups? He didn't answer me. Today he will surely say that the Civil Guard has a military character. If we already know and we have not proposed that I stop having it. It has nothing to do with it, but its internal mode of operation does not have to be identical to that of the Armed Forces. And above all, why is that agreement of the previous legislature not maintained in this one? Why does the government break it and change that general rule? Another big question, a mystery: why did you vote yes to Article 7bis in the previous legislature, because you agreed to this article to change it now? Not content with this, the Senate has adopted an amendment on Article 1.5 itself, an amendment on the Government's amendment; that is, they have amended themselves and in the Senate the Government has amended what it has brought here - because it does not seem hard enough, it is known - and what is coming now, what we are going to vote on this afternoon, is an amendment of the People's Party adopted in the Senate in which it is still more rejected, the application of the Military Penal Code to the Civil Guard is further tightened and leaves virtually no room for the non-application of the Code, practically in all circumstances the Military Penal Code will be applied, practically in all cases, except some that are clearly statistically minority. And I'm going to ask you the same thing again: why did they make that turn in the Senate about what came out here? Why do they do that? What has happened in a month so that you want to change your own Congress text in the Senate? What pressures have there been for them to do so and, if there have been such pressures, what have there been for such a surprising and surprising change to further harden the application of the Military Criminal Code to the Civil Guard? Perhaps in the background there is perhaps a compensatory element, perhaps. Since the deprivation of liberty has disappeared as a sanction in the disciplinary regime, we are going to raise it to the penal regime; since we cannot deprive it of liberty by disciplinary means because the sanctions of arrest have disappeared, we are going to increase the capacity to deprive it of liberty by criminal means; compensation or, better, an imbalance that makes no sense, because it would logically have been to accompany that philosophy with an adaptation and modulation of the application of the Military Penal Code to the Civil Guard. What they have done has been to further denaturalize the Civil Guard, to denaturalize it from its essential function, which is to preserve freedoms and rights and to guarantee citizen security. Basically, what is here is the expression of an authoritarian drift from the Ministry of the Interior, which has sponsored this change; an authoritarian drift that we have suffered in this legislature, especially in the last sections of it: Private Security Act, Citizen Security Act - the so-called gag law - and this very article 1.5 which further expands the cases of application of the Military Criminal Code to the Civil Guard. A maneuver, Minister, very clumsy.

Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, Minister, today the amendments introduced by the Senate in the Organic Law of the Military Penal Code, which are exclusively concerned with one subject, come to this House. In order to give concrete expression, I will tell you that the issue before you is not whether or not the Civil Guard is subject to military criminal law, but to what extent it is. I am already telling you, ladies and gentlemen, that we do not agree and this is obviously a problem, because in a country that built its way to democracy around the word consensus to the point of identifying the good policies with those that adhere to this concept, disagreement, the disparity of criteria has very bad press and worse image. However, that is, ladies and gentlemen, an opinion that I do not share, because consensus and dissent are the two sides of the same coin; the discrepancy is as legitimate and healthy for democracy as compromise is and, if I am in a hurry, it is the logical result of coexistence in this Chamber of disparate ideologies, which is the very essence of the parliamentary system. Having said that, I would like to convey to you my concern, which is that this will be the last debate we will have on the Military Penal Code; therefore, the last image that will be stuck in our retinas, and with this we could convey the feeling that there is a deep disagreement within this House regarding the Military Criminal Law, and nothing would be further from reality. For months we have negotiated to the point of exhaustion, and I would like to thank the spokesmen of the Socialist Party for their efforts in this regard. The consensus reached covers practically the whole of the law, with the exception of a single article, precisely the one that has been amended in the Senate. The result of this effort, ladies and gentlemen, the impulse of the Minister of Defence, has given rise to the most advanced Military Penal Code in the countries of our environment. It has been reduced to half by promoting its subsidiary character in relation to the Common Penal Code and yes, some types have been increased, Don Álvaro, and I will tell you which ones. Military jurisdiction had been reformed by transferring the appointment of military judges to the General Council of the Judiciary, which enshrined its independence. The prevention and punishment of violations of international humanitarian law has been included. The obligations arising from the ratification of the Statute of the International Criminal Court have been assumed. New criminal figures have been added for the criminal protection of the exercise of the rights and freedoms of the military. Sexual harassment, attacks on privacy, personal dignity and at work, and any kind of discriminatory act, have been criminalized. Conditions for the enforcement of sentences have also been improved, and a long time and so on. Therefore, ladies and gentlemen, there is a very broad consensus, and the Organic Law of the Military Penal Code is the daughter of that agreement supported by the majority of this House. Now, as I was telling you, we disagree in an article, one that defines the extent to which this penal code applies to members of the Civil Guard. Let me address two issues in this regard. First, it has been said that the People's Parliamentary Group has amended itself in the Senate, has amended the text submitted by the Government. Right, no doubt. But, in our view, that text has improved, it has contributed decisively to clarifying and completing its wording, and therefore the cases of application of this regulation to the members of the well-deserved institution, which in our view avoids confusion and contributes to legal certainty. But it has not done so in a whimsical manner, ladies and gentlemen, the new wording is better suited to the will of the legislator expressed in the third and fourth preambular paragraphs of the law, but above all it reflects and strictly adheres to the interpretation of the former Article 7bis by the Fifth Chamber and the Conflict Chamber of the Supreme Court, which in its repeated jurisprudence states that the Military Criminal Code must be applied at all times to members of the Civil Guard when they carry out acts affecting the legal assets of the Castrian order, mainly rooted in discipline, hierarchical relationship, internal unity and cohesion or the performance of functions and the fulfilment of essential duties which cannot be regulated in the police services. Secondly, ladies and gentlemen, there is a fundamental debate on this issue, and we cannot ignore the fact that a good number of the members of this august House claim that the Civil Guard loses its status as a military corps, loses its military character. Some would do so by royal decree from today to tomorrow and others are more likely to strip the institute gradually of all the characteristics and attributes that give it its military status until the matter falls as ripe fruit. In the subconscious or in the imaginary of one and the other lies the idea that the military character of the Civil Guard is an anomaly, an old Spanish singularity, something like the combs. Hard of what? Authoritarian regimes and dictatorships of the past. Ladies and gentlemen, it's a big mistake. Bodies that combine military and police status are a thing of these times, they are known as gendarmerie bodies and are so of these times that they are being empowered by all countries, including Spain, that have them. It does not constitute any anomaly, it does not constitute any Spanish singularity. A look at the nearest neighborhood allows us to observe analogous bodies, identical, in Portugal, France or Italy, and all serve fully and deeply democratic regimes and have their origin in them, as in the case of the Civil Guard. In fact, the only difference I see is that, in my modest opinion, the Civil Guard is the best gendarmerie body in the world. Do you know why in Italy or France you do not intend to withdraw the military character from the Gendarmerie or the Carabinieri? Precisely because, as in the case of the Civil Guard, his status as a military corps is in his own raison d'être, is his own raison d'être. It would make no sense to separate two police organizations that were exactly the same. Therefore, if these bodies were to lose their military character, they would be immediately absorbed by their corresponding police officers in each country and would simply disappear, which seems to be the goal of some. I will end by asking you the same questions as I asked you in June, and I will do so, ladies and gentlemen, because I consider them the fundamental question and because I am convinced that if you answer them with your hand in your heart today we will not have this debate. First, do you honestly believe that the citizens of this country want the Civil Guard to disappear? Do you think that is a majority sentiment among the people of our country? Are you really convinced that the civilian guards want the disappearance of your body? And, finally, do you think that we would be serving our country by depriving the state of an instrument like the Civil Guard? I beg you to answer them. Avoid prejudice, do it honestly and honestly, and you'll see how soon we're done with this discussion. Thank you very much. [[Applauds]]

ParlaMint-ES_2015-10-01-CD151001.u153	Thank you very much, Mr Guillaumes. For the Socialist Parliamentary Group, Mr Rodríguez Sánchez has the floor.
ParlaMint-ES_2015-10-01-CD151001.u154	Thank you very much, Madam President. I am speaking on behalf of the Socialist Group to establish a position on the amendments introduced in the Senate to the bill amending the Military Career Act. Before analyzing the changes introduced, it is appropriate to go back a bit and remember where the origin, scope and objective of this partial reform of the Military Career Act is, which today ends its process. This law originates from the mandate that was included in a final provision - the twelfth - of the Armed Forces Rights and Duties Act adopted in September 2011. It called for the Government to amend the issues covered by the transitional period of the Military Career Act, following the advice of the Defence Commission. In particular, it called for the analysis of issues such as promotion and change of scale, promotion regime, seniority, transition to reserve status, academic recognition, as well as the situation of staff on disability retirement. The first thing to note, therefore, is that we are dealing with a partial reform of the Military Career Act, which is confined to the transitional regime of the law and whose purpose is to correct certain dysfunctions detected in the application of the law and to give concrete expression to other aspects in the light of the experience of these years. A subcommission was set up within the Defence Commission to draw up the preliminary opinion provided for in the Act, where various appearances were held. Today, I would like again to acknowledge and thank the work that has been done and, in particular, the contributions of professional associations, experts, collectives, entities representing persons with disabilities, members of army headquarters and those responsible for the Ministry of Defence. As is well known, in our view the initial text of the opinion fell far short of the expectations of many groups and the Socialist Group itself. That is why we tabled a particular vote with twelve points with proposals which we thought should be included in that opinion. Finally, a more reasonable agreement could be reached in the final opinion that was adopted. Well, on the basis of that opinion and the proposals of our particular vote, we have drawn up the amendments that we have tried to incorporate into the draft law submitted by the Government. All these proposals have been made in the interests of the collectives and people affected by the transitional regime, but also taking into account the needs of the armies, the defence and the proper development of the Military Career Act. We have tried to do so as we try to address all the issues relating to defence: with the utmost responsibility and the utmost rigour. One point to remember is that an important part of the problems that led to the adoption of the Military Career Act are related to amendments that were adopted in this House for the transitional regime of the law that the previous Government referred. I would like to say with this in mind that in such complex matters as the military personnel regime and the development of the military career, it is first and foremost appropriate to increase prudence and measure the impact of what is proposed. Turning now to the amendments that come from the Senate, most of them relate to issues that had already been agreed in Congress and that were not included in the final opinion by omission or error. Some duplications have also been removed, such as in the case of honorary jobs, and some technical reforms or improvements have been incorporated that do not substantially alter what has been agreed in this House, with which there is no change of view or reason for the change of view by the Socialist Group. However, I do think it is appropriate in this process of finalizing the law, to refresh some of the changes that have been introduced in the draft law thanks to the amendments that we have tabled from the Socialist Group and from other groups and that have been negotiated with the People's Group in the process of Congress. First, we have strengthened our commitment to the internal promotion of the shift in the scale of non-commissioned officers. Our objective was to have a reserve of 20 per cent places for non-commissioned officers of the new officers' places. In any case, there is a clear mandate for internal promotion, in addition to something important, the Military Life Observatory will have to report on the implementation of these aspects. That is, we will know whether the processes of promotion of non-commissioned officers are working properly and whether there are difficulties in any army. There is no doubt that from the Socialist Group we will push all the necessary measures to ensure that these avenues of promotion work. On the other hand, through our amendments, we have managed to extend and introduce more favourable conditions for military personnel who are on reserve in application of the sixth, seventh and eighth transitional provisions of the Military Career Act. In the case of the seventh, which affects the non-commissioned officers, the criterion has been unified so that the pass to reserve and the promotion to lieutenant is at fifty-eight and fifty-six years, as the case may be, so that they do not suffer any hardship or loss of trienniums or passive rights even if they have remained in service longer. The award of the honorary promotion has also been expanded for those officers and non-commissioned officers who retire due to insufficient psychophysical conditions, although it has not occurred in an act of service. A provision has been included for the development of something referred to above, the regulation of second specialties of military personnel, which is important for the non-commissioned officers, for the career of the non-commissioned officer, but also for other cases, for example for staff with limitations, so that they can continue to operate within the Armed Forces. With regard to the military complements, an amendment had been introduced that promoted the ways in which they could remain in the Armed Forces in order to make better use of their experience and knowledge. Finally, since it has been mentioned in this rostrum, I would like to refer to a block of amendments which, although they did not specifically refer to the transitional arrangements, we did find it essential to introduce them. These are proposals concerning the prevention and prosecution of cases of harassment, abuse or sexual assault that contribute to and complement changes to the new disciplinary regime and the reform of the Military Penal Code. I'm going to refer to them. Section 101 of the Military Career Act has been amended to strengthen the protection of potential victims of these abuses, so that when the complaint is admitted or a disciplinary case is initiated, a change of unit may be requested. Article 92 has also been amended so that promotion assessment processes should report on the procedural status of the persons assessed. Finally, we have amended article 111 of the Military Career Act, so that an urgent procedure will be established by regulation to suspend military personnel in cases of allegations of harassment, abuse or sexual assault. I believe, Mr Sanz, that measures have been introduced and that they are incorporated into the law. We understand that these changes, together with the protocol for the prevention of sexual harassment and care for the victims of the Armed Forces, Minister - which we hope they will approve as soon as possible - as well as those amendments to the disciplinary regime of the Military Penal Code, are positive changes. We hope that they will serve to prevent, prevent and prosecute with complete force all crimes of harassment within the Armed Forces. For all these reasons, for all these changes that I have tried to summarize, my group is satisfied with the outcome of the process of law. Are we totally satisfied? Perhaps not entirely satisfied; we would have liked, among other things, to have included a provision on multiannual programming of places. We would also have liked to see strengthened mechanisms for monitoring and monitoring the evaluation processes and the personal reporting processes of military personnel. We would also have liked more concrete action on the issues of promotion of military personnel, as I have already pointed out, and we would have been more in line if mention had been made of the equalization of the CNI officers in relation to the rest of their colleagues in matters affecting the transition to the reserve. Finally, we would have liked this information process on the promotion of non-commissioned officers to have been incorporated into the Act on the Rights and Duties of Members of the Armed Forces and would not have become a mere additional provision of this Act. This is all part of the negotiation and we are aware of the majority in the House. Despite all this, I insist, we believe that significant progress has been made in the transitional regime, which was the subject of the law, with regard to the initial draft that the Government referred. That said, the job doesn't end here. We are aware of the work of modernization and transformation of the Armed Forces that have made governments of different signs since the recovery of democracy, with a clear objective: that our country has modern armies capable of securing our defence and our security. These are changes and transformations that we value positively, but we also know that it is necessary to continue in that process, that things remain to be done. In this spirit we have tried to contribute to this partial improvement of the Military Career Act and will continue to address as many reforms as necessary in the future. Thank you very much, Madam President. [[Applauds]]

For this reason, it is fair to thank the efforts and eagerness of the political groups to achieve a result that seeks to prolong and improve the adverse effects generated by the Military Race Act 39/2007. As has been said on numerous occasions, after many avatars, because of the complexity and high degree of controversy that occurred both among the military groups and between the opposition, the parliamentarians who handled it in 2007 stated that they had received a huge number of mails and requests for meetings from persons affected by this law. The difficulty of processing is clear from the fact that it took a year from the bill's inception until it was finally passed, and in this case thirty thousand contentious-administrative resources were generated. There is no need for great mental brilliance to know that it was not very popular among its recipients and neither did it seem to us a satisfactory law. That law was born, in our opinion, without having matured the necessary to come to an end. It lacked the necessary study and reflection for such an arid and important matter, possibly motivated by the excessive legislative zeal on the part of the PSOE in matters relating to the Armed Forces, especially with the desire to change what was done in previous legislatures. This law was the third that decisively influenced the career expectations of members of the Armed Forces in just over fifteen years. This 2007 rule generated uncertainty, mistrust and professional disorientation, as well as insecurity and frustration among members of the Armed Forces and also among those who intended to be. Three years after the entry into force and given the disastrous nature of this law, the People's Group, taking advantage of the processing of Organic Law 9/2011, on the rights and duties of members of the Armed Forces, to which the spokesman of the Socialist Group referred, made it a condition that it should include a six-month period in which the Government would refer to the Congress of Deputies a draft law for the reform of the transitional regime of the Military Career Act, for which the Commission for the Defence of Congress should issue an opinion on a preliminary basis that would address the different elements of the transitional period of the law. But the dissolution of the General Courts prevented this change from taking place within the required time frame, while the economic crisis and the risk of intervention of Spain made this priority a second place, so the creation of a subcommission for the reform of the transitional regime of the Military Career Act was delayed until November 2012. The subcommission's work was very hard. The sessions were expanded several times, so that we could hear - as has already been said in this rostrum - all the parties involved and have the best possible visions. The final report of the subcommission was approved by the Defence Commission on 9 September 2014 by a large majority. Although initially the People's Group intended to amend Law 39/2007 of the Military Race, the time that this law had been in force, which was almost seven years, led to the fact that if it were finally decided to draw up a new one, it could fall into the same defects that it had fallen into in 2007. At the same time, the rights acquired during these seven years in force threatened to cause another 30,000 disputes, and if the law was changed, it did not guarantee that the 30,000 previous disputes that already existed would be resolved. The threat of chaos in the Armed Forces, as well as the need to give professional stability to the military, has led the People's Group to take the decision not to draft a new law, but to modify the existing one in those main points and in those areas where it was obliged to do so by the mandate of Organic Law 9/2011. In this regard, efforts have been made to reach the maximum consensus, both in the drafting of the draft law and in the previous meetings of the subcommission. I take this opportunity to thank the groups for their effectiveness and also for the great effort that has been made, which has resulted in a bill to reform the Military Career Act, which I insist, although it does not solve all the problems created or all the injustices that could arise from the emergence of the said Act, it does bring important improvements, as has already been said in this rostrum. But I will insist that it is very important that we have made a battery of measures to support victims of sexual harassment, a subject on which all groups are particularly aware; that military personnel are effectively linked to retirees in special circumstances through their voluntary assignment to the Armed Forces, allowing them to achieve successive honorary promotions with special consideration for the act of service; that retired non-commissioned officers who are unable to avail themselves of the tenth additional provision of the Military Career Act are allowed to do so; that the conditions for promotion as they move to the reserve situation, adjusting them in accordance with the experience of implementation since the entry into force of the law both of officers and of non-commissioned officers; that the deadline for fulfilling the requirement of more than thirty-three years since entry into the Armed Forces for the voluntary transition to the reserve is extended by two years for certain bodies and scales, and that the ambiguity of the previous text is clarified. Access to the permanent status of the complement military is facilitated. Staff who went directly to the retirement situation without going through the reserve situation are granted an honorary job, provided that, when they moved to retirement, they had twenty years of service since becoming a professional soldier and, in any case, for those who did so in the course of their service. It is also worth noting the recovery of casualties in training centres, the assessment of merits in training processes, the need for specialization on the basis of certain jobs, the need to regulate regularly aspects related to assessments and procedures for the transition to the situation of suspension of duties, the provision of destination and commissions in exceptional circumstances, the unification of criteria in the resolutions of resources related to psychophysical conditions files, the system of calculation of periods of casualties, the moment of acquisition of the status of military troop and navy. However, ladies and gentlemen, I reiterate once again that we are aware that many things are still outstanding; that there are problems to be solved, that there are just concerns and that they have not been satisfied. Many of these issues can be resolved through management, which is the third step to which we are committed, and we will continue with the work that allows us to comply with those claims that, being fair and reasonable, did not require their inclusion in the legal text. But today we can say that we have fulfilled the legal mandate by collecting the Organic Law 9/2011, on the rights and duties of members of the Armed Forces, as well as the objective pursued, which is nothing but a positive result for the military and for our Armed Forces. ParlaMint-ES_2015-10-01-CD151001.u161	Thank you very much, Madam President. With regard to these Senate amendments, which put an end to the legislative iter of another important bill, we have to quote Jöel de Rosnay again. We have quoted him in the debate on the Criminal Prosecution Act and we have to quote again his reflections on what he calls with relevance and lucid political fluidity, political fluidity. In his reinterpretation here in the Congress of the Deputies Don Cayo Lara has redefined it as liquid politics, that is, politics without foundations, without requirements, without solidity and without acceptance of the requirements of the dogma of the legal order. Unfortunately, we have to be very critical of this bill. On the part of the People's Parliamentary Group in Congress - I don't see any minister, although surely there will be a person who will reply to me and then quote me - it is usually done very often or with recurrence manifestly false invocations. It is often said that we live in the most centralized state in the European Union. Formally, ours can be a centralized State and the Constitution provides for and enshrines the operative principle, in Title VIII, which distributes powers between the autonomous communities and the State, articles 148 and 149, but this is merely a formal and moreover false invocation. The degradation of the autonomous process has been of such magnitude and through so many instruments that we really live in an almost unitary and highly centralized State. What are the procedures? Multiple. Such as disorbiting the bases of the State, when the State has the competence to dictate the bases of the legislative development powers of the autonomous communities. To overstate the framework legislation of the State when it can correlate the legislative powers of the autonomous communities. Overwhelming the possibilities of intervention of what is called high State inspection, which, being a meta-legal concept, nevertheless works very efficiently to cut down on competences. To detract from the narrow meta-legal concept of the general guiding principles of the economy; concepts that are not part of the legal system but are para-legal, such as budgetary stability, by virtue of rules of stability imposed by the European Union that are not a source of law but nevertheless correlate and reduce the competence of the autonomous communities. And the CORA, Madam President. CORA is the true exterminator angel of the device principle that configures the legal order. Everything that has been born and is the son of the CORA constitutes a competitive invasion and a reduction of the competitive acquis attributed by the Spanish Constitution itself and its statutes of autonomy, ultimately by the block of constitutionality, to the autonomous communities. We see this with particular brutality or with particular clarity, by using a more forensic parliamentary expression, in this law. This law, with regard to the CORA and the requirements of the configuration of a legal regime for public administrations, does not refer to the public administrations over which this institution, the General Courts of the State, could be competent, the State public administrations and their corporate bodies, their institutional bodies, their organs or corporations governed by public law, semi-public law and their public societies, but also to the autonomous communities, and does so even in violation of the jurisprudence of the Constitutional Court. The jurisprudence of the Constitutional Court - I am referring to the amendment number 107 of our group in the Senate to article 49 - is the true paradigm and clarifies what happens with a law of these characteristics. The amendment does not deny that the State, through article 149.1.18 (a) of the Constitution, has exclusive powers to determine the basis for the functioning of public administrations, not the basis for the capacity of the autonomous public administrations to set up institutional bodies, but their functioning. In fact, and reproducing the expression of the Constitutional Court in its foundation No. 13, Legal basis for public administrations, we must remember that in its organizational and operational aspects all public administrations must accept what the State dictates in order to determine, if not exclusively, principles which must be developed by the autonomous communities and which guarantee the unity or equality of citizens and the functioning of the administrations throughout the Spanish State. However, the Constitutional Court itself has refuted this jurisdictional criterion which it confers on the State in respect of the legal regime of public administrations in relation to its capacity to establish basic principles and rules on the organizational aspects and functioning of all public administrations, with other subsequent judgments, in particular Judgement 49/1988, 225/1993, 197/1996 and others, whose legal bases it might cite, Madam President, stating the following: The State may establish the common normative denominator that encloses the bases and from which each autonomous community with legislative development powers can regulate the matter according to its peculiarities and interests, but it cannot do so with such a degree of detail and in such a complete or complete way that it practically prevents the adoption by the autonomous communities of its own policies in this field by exercising its legislative development powers. This is what happens with this law, Madam President. The determination of the bases of the State in terms of the organization and functioning of public administrations, so finished, so prolific and so developed that the normative competence that the Spanish Constitution itself confers on the autonomous communities, remains empty of content, is completely sterilized. And since in this law everything is inspired by this disturbing principle of constitutional jurisprudence - see, a group like ours that is a reference of authority is using constitutional jurisprudence - we will vote negatively on all the amendments tabled by the Senate in relation to this bill. There are other issues that deserve to be considered, although I will do so more briefly than the previous time, Madam President. Why and on what basis should agreements between the Autonomous Communities and each other or with the State be referred to the Senate for assessment and evaluation? But aren't we almost all in agreement here that the Senate is a second reading chamber and that perhaps the best thing to do with it is its political amortization? Are we going to assign you such relevant powers as the evaluation and regulatory control of agreements signed between the Autonomous Communities? [[The Lord]] This, of course, deserves much deeper reflection than the mandate contained in this bill. Finally, we are going to ask for something that is recurrent and that we always ask that we have the opportunity to do so, which is the amendment of articles 23 to 25 of the current Lofage, of Law 6/1997. In short, what are we asking? The disappearance of the delegations of the Government and the sub-delegates. It makes no sense at this point in history that there is an overlap of competent bodies, some State delegates for the powers that the State has not transferred, and others that hold the high representation of the State by virtue of an attribution of a constitutional character. The delegate of the Government is provided for in the Constitution, it is true - article 154 - but the role of ordinary representative of the State in each autonomous community is also assigned to its president - to the lehendakari, to the president, to whom it results by virtue of the relevant denomination - who is the president of that autonomous community, article 152.1 of the Spanish Constitution and in the case of Euskadi, article 33.2 of the Statute of Autonomy of the Basque Country. Moreover, the nature of the facts has already made it relevant to avoid duplication - and this is the role of the CORA - that in a system such as that of the Spanish State, which is increasingly operating under territorial conferences, sectoral conferences between the political leaders of the autonomous communities and of the General Administration of the State, these figures, those of the delegate of the Government and the deputy delegate of the Government, are losing increasing legal relevance and are only causing, if possible, distortions and dysfunctions in order to articulate the policies corresponding to each other. From this perspective, and on the basis of these two amendments and some more technical in nature in relation to the configuration of the notice and in relation to the incorporation into the laws of norms with normative effect and other legal technical deficiencies that it would be very difficult to report, Mr President, I conclude here by expressing our profound criticism of a particularly intrusive law competitively, contradicting the jurisprudence of the Constitutional Court. With this bill, a fully electronic, zero-paper and interconnected public administration is involved. The creation of new bodies may only be made after verification that there are no other bodies. Transparency is increased by creating new public administrative registers, so that any citizen or company can know which public entities exist. The administrative structure is further streamlined and for the first time a system is established for the supervision, control, evaluation and extinction of public bodies. Information between administrations is improved to avoid duplication, to improve coordination. In short, it has an impact on greater control, on greater discipline of administrations in order to strengthen fiscal consolidation. ParlaMint-ES_2015-10-01-CD151001.u179	Thank you very much, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, this law sets out the position before the Spanish courts of third countries, international organizations, conferences and meetings held in our territory, and also of heads of State and foreign ministers. It also provides for the immunity of warships and ships and aircraft from other States, as well as the Armed Forces belonging to other countries that are in Spain or transit through our territory in a transitory manner. It is a law that culminates in a legislative package that has taken place in this legislature and that has been promoted by the PP Government: the Law on Foreign Action, the Treaty Act, the Regulation of the Diplomatic Career and, in this case, this law, processed by the urgency procedure, without specifying that urgency - then I will return to it -.
