As with previous laws, the Foreign Action Act and the Treaty Act, a great opportunity has been missed to legislate allowing for greater participation of these Chambers in the design of this country's foreign policy. So we have a minister, Margallo, a legislator, but also a little dialogue minister or the roller minister, as my colleague, Mr Àlex Sáez Jubero, who is at the Council of Europe today in Strasbourg, would say. So we have tabled an amendment, we have withdrawn it and then in the Commission, seeing that the final draft included many of the recommendations made by both the Council of State and the General Council of the Judiciary, we have decided to support this law. This correction demonstrates the risk that is assumed when this Government legislates without consultation and without responding to the mandatory opinions claiming urgency when there is not, as it has done with other texts, such as the one we discussed this morning on the reform of the Constitutional Court. The need was dubious and there was no compelling cause for it to be forced to do so on an emergency basis. It is clear that there were justified reasons, for example, that the United Nations Convention on Immunities of 2 December 2004, as recognized by the Council of the Judiciary itself, is not in force and appears not to be in force for many years because only seventeen States have ratified it. That is why, as the Council recommended, once this treaty comes into force the law itself should provide for its withdrawal, and the Government has also included this. Again, there is a recommendation that improves the project and demonstrates the hastyness of doing so without taking into account the preceptive opinions from the outset. We have tabled a number of amendments, many of them technical ones, some of which I would like to highlight. Firstly, Amendment No 14, on which the Group of the European People's Party has submitted a compromise that we are going to accept. This is an amendment that seeks to distinguish immunity from jurisdiction from immunity from enforcement, so that the judges and courts of one State cannot be tried in another State and so that the trial for States and persons with that guarantee is not enforced. Although we accept this amendment, gentlemen of the PP, this compromise amendment does not contribute anything, since as the law establishes it can only regulate immunities in Spain and not in third countries. Therefore, you have good will, but your amendment contains no substance in the text of our initial amendment, so I move on to the next amendment. You also accept Amendment No 20, which is important but does not limit, as you also said, the permanence of responsibility, for acts prior to taking office, to criminal responsibility. We have another amendment, number 17, which requires that civil liability should not lapse when there are punishable offences; it is intended that when immunity occurs that does not mean that States cannot assume civil liability for crimes for which immunity has effect - this is an important issue. Amendment 18, which we keep alive, proposes to include the offences of terrorism and corruption as conduct that cannot fall under immunity for acts inherent in the exercise of its functions; terrorism and corruption, crimes that do not appear in the original text. I am also very interested in Amendment No 21, which would require that, if parliamentary control is accepted, decisions are taken to allow troops from non-NATO third countries to be in our country. We are not asking for ex ante parliamentary control but ex post and, of course, for important decisions on foreign policy and security policy, in order to go, as we have asked throughout the legislature, making progress in the democratization of foreign policy, something that has not happened with this government; we have retreated. With Amendment No 17, we propose the creation of a compensation fund, so that such civil compensations can take effect and be exercised when they take place. Finally, we introduce Amendment 24 on universal jurisdiction. This issue of universal justice is not just one of my group. Even the General Council of the Judiciary recommends that the absolute destruction that has been done in this area since the adoption of Law 1/2014 - I would like to recall the debate in this House on 16 December in which my colleague, Mrs Soraya Rodríguez, defended the urgent repeal of this reform - has placed us, as a country, in an unacceptable situation of backwardness in the fight against human rights violations and with impairment of universal jurisdiction. This must be corrected, because we are a country in which we can no longer speak of defending human rights, but of situations of impunity when very serious crimes are committed against humanity. The reform has placed our country in a worse position than the pre-democracy; crimes such as genocide, against humanity and against property and persons in the event of armed conflict can go unpunished because their persecution is severely limited. The same applies to crimes of gender-based violence, sexual integrity, trafficking, apart from those that affect minors with serious attacks on their integrity, such as genital mutilation, for which you have included a requirement of persecubility that makes it very difficult. Therefore, this amendment is fundamental and we very much regret that this legislation is not being used to rectify the very serious damage done with Law 1/2014. Finally, we are told that you accept Amendment 25, which would include in the explanatory statement that parliamentary control of external action should be strengthened; however, this amendment has no effect because in the only article in which such parliamentary control is introduced - Article 21 - you do not accept it. With this, it makes no sense to speak in the statement of reasons for strengthening parliamentary control and then in the law to forget about this issue. It's a toast to the sun; honestly, it's something very unserious. I'm finishing up. ParlaMint-ES_2015-10-01-CD151001.u183	Thank you, President. This project has a long name, and I will repeat it because it is worth reading again. You know that the arrest of General Augusto Pinochet for British justice in London on 17 October 1998 to initiate a process of extradition to Spain for war crimes and violations of fundamental rights marked a real milestone in what has been called the internationalization of criminal justice. It was a very specific situation of what we are now regulating: a head of State - in this case a former head of State - who visited a foreign country and found himself with a legal application based on the crimes claimed in his country of origin. Well, it is important that we enjoy an article 29 - and I say it with joy - because, no doubt, if an article of these characteristics were not in the law, it could not be voted on by my parliamentary group, and the spokesman for the Socialist Group referred to it in the past. He said that the provisions of the present title would not affect Spain's international obligations with regard to the prosecution of international crimes. Therefore, although it is true, it is an excessively lax wording, we have considered it positive that it is at least present in the drafting of the law. It is also said that it will not affect Spain's commitments to the International Criminal Court. Therefore, we welcome regulation - we think it is positive - and we also welcome this exception without which we could not understand that people affected by international crimes could walk freely through our country, despite the many immunities they have in their legal status. Thank you very much.
ParlaMint-ES_2015-10-01-CD151001.u184	Thank you very much, Mr. Nuet. For the People's Group, Mr Beneyto has the floor.
ParlaMint-ES_2015-10-01-CD151001.u185	Thank you very much, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, with this law of such a technical appearance, it is becoming a corollary to what has been a very complete legislative programme throughout this parliamentary term. It has been the legislative period in which more laws have been adopted in the field of Spanish foreign policy. In fact, the legislative framework in which the Spanish foreign policy will have to be moved from now on has been completely re-created: the Law on Foreign Action and Service of the State, the Treaty Act, the foreign action strategy, which is going to mean permanent control by this House over foreign policy, is progressing in the parliamentaryization of foreign policy in a very remarkable way, and today we bring to this House - the Government with the support of the People's Parliamentary Group - the law on immunities and jurisdiction. I would like to point out that all the legislative initiatives, all the activity carried out by the Committee on Foreign Affairs in this House, the activity carried out by the Government has always, absolutely always, been presided over by the idea of obtaining maximum consensus with the parliamentary groups. All the laws have been the subject of a thorough analysis and discussion with the parliamentary groups in this House; we have taken into account, where reasonably possible, the amendments tabled by the opposition groups and, again in this law, I am pleased that we can accept some of the Socialist Group's amendments - paragraphs 19, 20 and 25 - and that we can have a compromise amendment with paragraph 14, also tabled by the Socialist Group. The emergency procedure is simply for the law to be passed; it obviously has no other functionality. It is also very important to have heard, as it could not otherwise be, the mandatory opinions of the consultative bodies which, as has been pointed out by the spokespersons of the other groups, have had timely recognition and acceptance in the text finally presented. Let me briefly point out some of the main points of this law. It fills a gap in the Spanish legal system that to date contained a generic revision of international law and customary rules. It responds to the Constitutional Court's recommendation for greater legal certainty. It determines in a concrete way the position before the Spanish courts of foreign States, international organizations with offices or offices in Spain, international conferences and meetings to be held in our country, heads of State and Government and foreign ministers of other countries, warships and vessels and aircraft belonging to the State and armed forces belonging to another State that are located or transit through Spanish territory. Therefore, what it introduces is clarity, as it has always wanted and carried out this parliamentary group throughout this legislature; clarity, precision, accuracy, respect for international legality at all times, taking into account the conventions, primarily the United Nations Convention, which has not, however, entered into force, as it is known. The Government completed the normative framework on international law, improved legal certainty for citizens and facilitated the work of judges. It also improves the image of Spain as a country, following in this to other countries in our environment, with a clear, predictable and solvent legal framework, favoring the installation of offices and headquarters of international agencies and the collaboration of their Armed Forces with those of other countries. It should be borne in mind, as I pointed out, that there is no regulatory instrument governing the matter. There are only generic references to international law and customary rules that do not provide a satisfactory response and lead to dysfunctions in practice. The purpose of this law is to ensure full compliance with international law. It does not confer new privileges or immunities, but rather collects existing ones. The obligations acquired by Spain in the international sphere are incorporated into domestic law, incorporating the provisions of the 2004 United Nations Convention on Jurisdictional Immunities of States and Their Property, ratified by Spain but which has not entered into force; it must be ratified by thirty States and it does not appear to enter into force at an early date. It also includes everything that has not been legislated to date: immunities of the foreign State and its representatives, other immunities arising from the intensity of cooperation between countries such as visiting armed forces, international agencies, ships and aircraft, as well as international conferences and meetings. It regulates immunities between the Spanish courts, based on a clear doctrine: that the equals do not have jurisdiction over each other, par in parem non habet imperium, which applies both to jurisdiction and enforcement. This principle, well established in international law and derived from the sovereignty and sovereign equality of States, initially recognizes immunities in favour of States and has been extended to other subjects such as international organizations and international conferences and meetings. Logically, matters that are regulated by widely accepted international treaties to which Spain is a party, especially the two major international treaties on immunities, namely the 1961 Vienna Convention on Diplomatic Relations and the 1963 Vienna Convention on Consular Relations, remain outside the law. Therefore, ladies and gentlemen, the codification in a single, systematic and coherent text of the legal regime for immunities will facilitate its correct application by Spanish legal operators and the fulfilment for Spain of its international obligations. The Government thus completes the normative framework in the area of international law to which they have referred to the principle, ensures certainty in the domestic jurisdictional sphere, is homologated with what has been achieved in other countries such as the United States, Canada, the United Kingdom, Switzerland, Australia and Japan, which already possess its law, and all this is done with full respect for international obligations and according to the approach estimated by the Constitutional Court. In short, it is a clear improvement in the image of Spain as an active State in international and legally secure, predictable and solvent matters, comparable to the most advanced States. Throughout the legislature, the People's Parliamentary Group and the Government, which is supported by the People's Parliamentary Group, have only contributed to our country's systematic and efficient response to the more current development of international law. We have achieved a new legislative framework and have succeeded in consolidating a number of very key international relations for our country with key countries, such as the improvement of relations with the United States, the consolidation of excellent relations with Morocco, the privileged relationship with some Latin American countries such as the countries of the Pacific Alliance, the continuation of the opening up of the countries of South-East Asia with our improvement - the dual year showed - for example, relations with Japan, and our very active presence as members of the Permanent Council of the United Nations. Therefore, ladies and gentlemen, this law puts the end to a legislature that has been characterized by great activity, a desire for consensus at all times and a clear achievement of results in the improvement of Spain's international relations. Thank you very much. [[Applauds]]
ParlaMint-ES_2015-10-01-CD151001.u186	Thank you very much, Mr. Beneyto. There has been an anomaly for which I am partly responsible. I thought that there was no representative of the Catalan Parliamentary Group of Convergence and Unity who wanted to speak, and that is not the case. Therefore, Mr Guillaumes has the floor.
ParlaMint-ES_2015-10-01-CD151001.u187	Thank you, Mr. President. I'll try to make it up to you soon. It is not a minor issue to regulate matters at the legal level because these regulations, as someone has said, already existed, but everything that has to do with international headquarters, diplomatic meetings, etc. must be brought to the legal level. Because the truth is that any country, any State has the legitimate desire to become the seat of international bodies, international meetings and international conferences and it generates a kind of, we could say, free competition in the worst sense of the word in this case; a free competition that consists in lowering very often the list of requirements in terms of diplomatic immunities, etc. Therefore, welcome is a law that can deprive any State of such temptations. We understand that the law already incorporated an improvement with the report of the Ministry of Foreign Affairs and Cooperation in all judicial matters that are pending between the Spanish State and the States that come to such an event. There are other issues that have been incorporated through the Council of State, partly through amendment, but we would have liked judicial notifications to be made between States, a clear determination - this is in part - of which person has diplomatic immunity or not, the identification - this would be important - of which assets of a foreign embassy or foreign State are likely to be embargoed, or, finally, when the State Advocate must act against a foreign representative or international body in a judicial process. In short, many things are regulated; before it was very dispersed and in rules without legislative rank.
